# Delocalization of bias in unadjusted Hamiltonian Monte Carlo and underdamped Langevin

**arXiv ID**: 2607.15208v1
**Authors**: Yifan Chen, Xiaoou Cheng, Jonathan Niles-Weed, Jonathan Weare
**Published**: 2026-07-16
**Categories**: stat.CO, cs.LG, math.PR, stat.ML
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.15208v1

## Abstract

Unadjusted samplers such as unadjusted Hamiltonian Monte Carlo and underdamped Langevin are well-known to be biased. Metropolis--Hastings adjustment has been conventionally incorporated into Hamiltonian Monte Carlo to eliminate the bias. However, this adjustment can significantly increase the iteration complexity due to the small step size required for reasonable Metropolis acceptance rates. In this work, we extend the \emph{delocalization of bias} phenomenon, previously established for the overdamped Langevin algorithm, to these two unadjusted algorithms. We show that to control the $W_2$ bias of any $K$-dimensional marginal of a high-dimensional distribution, $O(\sqrt{K})$ integration steps suffice up to $\log d$ terms, assuming either weak or sparse interactions among variables. The discrete-time integrators here introduce technical difficulties beyond those of the overdamped setting, which we address through a broadly applicable matrix-polynomial framework that characterizes their propagators. Our result for the underdamped Langevin algorithm is valid for all large friction parameters, implying that the Leimkuhler-Matthews integrator for the overdamped Langevin dynamics also exhibits delocalization of bias.

Delocalization of bias in unadjusted Hamiltonian Monte Carlo and underdamped Langevin

## Delocalization of bias in unadjusted Hamiltonian Monte Carlo and underdamped LangevinYifan Chen†,Xiaoou Cheng‡,Jonathan Niles-Weed‡andJonathan Weare‡†Department of Mathematics, University of California, Los Angeles, CA 90095, USA‡Courant Institute, New York University, NY 10012, USAyifanchen@math.ucla.edu, chengxo@nyu.edu, jnw@cims.nyu.edu, weare@nyu.edu

## Abstract.

Unadjusted samplers such as unadjusted Hamiltonian Monte Carlo and underdamped Langevin are well-known to be biased. Metropolis–Hastings adjustment has been conventionally incorporated into Hamiltonian Monte Carlo to eliminate the bias. However, this adjustment can significantly increase the iteration complexity due to the small step size required for reasonable Metropolis acceptance rates. In this work, we extend thedelocalization of biasphenomenon, previously established for the overdamped
Langevin algorithm, to these two unadjusted algorithms. We show that to control theW2W_{2}bias of anyKK-dimensional marginal of a high-dimensional distribution,O​(K)O(\sqrt{K})integration steps suffice up tolog⁡d\log dterms, assuming either weak or sparse interactions among variables. The discrete-time integrators here introduce technical difficulties beyond those of the overdamped setting, which we address through a broadly applicable matrix-polynomial framework that characterizes their propagators. Our result for the underdamped Langevin algorithm is valid for all large friction parameters, implying that the Leimkuhler-Matthews integrator for the overdamped Langevin dynamics also exhibits delocalization of bias.

## 1.Introduction

Hamiltonian (or Hybrid) Monte Carlo (HMC) and underdamped Langevin (UL)111Note that the unadjusted Langevin algorithm (ULA) is instead a discretization of theoverdampedLangevin dynamics.are widely used to sample from high dimensional probability distributions. For a target distributionπ∝exp⁡(−V)\pi\propto\exp(-V), whereV:ℝd→ℝV:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}denotes the potential energy, both sampling dynamics evolve in terms of a position variableqq, on which the potentialVVis defined, and an auxiliary momentum variablepp. HMC relies on the Hamiltonian ordinary differential equation(1.1)d​qtd​t=pt,\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}q_{t}}{{\rm d}t}=p_{t}\,,d​ptd​t=−∇V​(qt).\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}p_{t}}{{\rm d}t}=-\nabla V(q_{t})\,.

corresponding to the
Hamiltonianℋ​(q,p)=V​(q)+|p|ℓ22/2\mathcal{H}(q,p)=V(q)+|p|_{\ell^{2}}^{2}/2, where|⋅|ℓ2|\cdot|_{\ell^{2}}is theℓ2\ell^{2}norm of a vector.
The closely related continuous-time UL is described by the stochastic differential equation(1.2)d​qt=pt​d​t,\displaystyle{\rm d}q_{t}=p_{t}{\rm d}t\,,d​pt=−∇V​(qt)​d​t−γ​pt​d​t+2​γ​d​Bt.\displaystyle{\rm d}p_{t}=-\nabla V(q_{t}){\rm d}t-\gamma p_{t}{\rm d}t+\sqrt{2\gamma}{\rm d}B_{t}\,.

whereγ>0\gamma>0is the friction parameter andBtB_{t}is a standard Brownian
motion. Both continuous-time dynamics admitπ⊗𝒩​(0,I)\pi\otimes\mathcal{N}(0,I)as their invariant measure on phase space(q,p)(q,p), allowing samplers built upon them to targetπ\pivia theqq-marginal.

Applying these continuous-time dynamics numerically requires time discretization. For HMC, one must also refresh the momentum regularly to ensure sampling ergodicity. The most commonly used discretization of (1.1)
is the leap-frog scheme (also called velocity Verlet)[31], under which the(k+1)(k+1)-th step of HMC is

Samplep0∼𝒩​(0,I)p_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)and setq0=Xkq_{0}=X_{k}.
- (2)

Inner loop (leap-frog integration): forj=0j=0tom−1m-1,(1.3)qj+1\displaystyle q_{j+1}=qj+h​pj−h22​∇V​(qj),\displaystyle=q_{j}+hp_{j}-\frac{h^{2}}{2}\nabla V(q_{j})\,,pj+1\displaystyle p_{j+1}=pj−h2​∇V​(qj)−h2​∇V​(qj+1).\displaystyle=p_{j}-\frac{h}{2}\nabla V(q_{j})-\frac{h}{2}\nabla V(q_{j+1})\,.

SetXk+1=qmX_{k+1}=q_{m}.

Here,hhis the step size, and we also refer to one HMC iteration as one outer loop. For (1.2), a commonly used discretization is the BAOAB scheme[31], which is written as(1.4)qk+1\displaystyle q_{k+1}=qk+h2​(1+η)​pk−h24​(1+η)​∇V​(qk)+h2​1−η2​ξk,\displaystyle=q_{k}+\frac{h}{2}(1+\eta)p_{k}-\frac{h^{2}}{4}(1+\eta)\nabla V(q_{k})+\frac{h}{2}\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}}\xi_{k}\,,pk+1\displaystyle p_{k+1}=η​(pk−h2​∇V​(qk))+1−η2​ξk−h2​∇V​(qk+1).\displaystyle=\eta(p_{k}-\frac{h}{2}\nabla V(q_{k}))+\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}}\xi_{k}-\frac{h}{2}\nabla V(q_{k+1})\,.

The scheme (1.4) is constructed through an operator splitting. We denote the solution maps for the three components of (1.2) over a step sizehhas follows:(1.5)𝖡h​(q,p)=(q,p−h​∇V​(q)),\displaystyle{\mathsf{B}_{h}}(q,p)=(q,p-h\nabla V(q))\,,𝖠h​(q,p)=(q+h​p,p),\displaystyle{\mathsf{A}_{h}}(q,p)=(q+hp,p)\,,𝖮h​(q,p;ξ)=(q,η​p+1−η2​ξ).\displaystyle{\mathsf{O}_{h}}(q,p;\xi)=(q,\eta p+\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}}\xi)\,.

Here,ξ∼𝒩​(0,I)\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)is an independent Gaussian vector, andη=exp⁡(−γ​h)\eta=\exp(-\gamma h). Splitting methods for underdamped Langevin dynamics are defined with different strings of A, B, and O. By standard convention, operators employed symmetrically in the string are applied with a half steph/2h/2, while those that appear only once are applied with a full stephh. Specifically, the BAOAB scheme (1.4) is derived from the composition𝖡h/2∘𝖠h/2∘𝖮h​(⋅;ξ)∘𝖠h/2∘𝖡h/2\mathsf{B}_{h/2}\circ\mathsf{A}_{h/2}\circ\mathsf{O}_{h}(\cdot;\xi)\circ\mathsf{A}_{h/2}\circ\mathsf{B}_{h/2}, which we abbreviate as𝖡𝖠𝖮𝖠𝖡\mathsf{B}\mathsf{A}\mathsf{O}\mathsf{A}\mathsf{B}. For the frictionless caseγ=0\gamma=0, we haveη=1\eta=1, so the𝖮h\mathsf{O}_{h}step becomes the identity,
and the BAOAB scheme (1.4) reduces to the leap-frog scheme (1.3).

## 1.1.Motivation

The unadjusted HMC and UL algorithms are biased. Because of the finite step sizehh, when the marginalρk\rho_{k}ofqkq_{k}converges, its limitπh\pi_{h}differs from
the targetπ\pi. Both HMC and UL can be modified with a Metropolis–Hastings (MH) accept/reject step to eliminate bias. In fact, HMC is most frequently applied with MH adjustment[42,13,47]. However, adjusted HMC and UL are well known to degrade asddincreases and the step sizehhmust be decreased to maintain a non-zero acceptance probability.

In[17], we have identified a “delocalization of bias” phenomenon for the unadjustedoverdampedLangevin algorithm: To control the bias of low-dimensional marginals, a step size scaling with the low dimension suffices, with only a logarithmic dependence on the full dimensiondd.
The analysis in[17]relies on a novelW2,ℓ∞W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}metric, which captures the accuracy of low-dimensional marginals. For two probability measuresμ\muandν\nuonℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, theirW2,ℓ∞W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}distance is defined as(1.6)W2,ℓ∞​(μ,ν)=(minγ∈Π​(μ,ν)​∫|x−y|ℓ∞2​γ​(d​x,d​y))1/2,W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}(\mu,\nu)=\left(\min_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\int|x-y|^{2}_{\ell^{\infty}}\gamma({\rm d}x,{\rm d}y)\right)^{1/2}\,,

where|⋅|ℓ∞|\cdot|_{\ell^{\infty}}is theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}norm of a vector andΠ​(μ,ν)\Pi(\mu,\nu)represents the set of measures on the joint spaceℝd×ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}that have marginalsμ\muandν\nu. We note that, for any choice ofKKcoordinates, the corresponding marginal distributionsμ(K)\mu^{(K)}andν(K)\nu^{(K)}ofμ\muandν\nu, satisfy the upper boundW2​(μ(K),ν(K))≤K​W2,ℓ∞​(μ,ν)W_{2}(\mu^{(K)},\nu^{(K)})\leq\sqrt{K}W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}(\mu,\nu), sinceK​|x−y|ℓ∞2≥∑t=1K|x(jt)−y(jt)|2K|x-y|_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2}\geq\sum_{t=1}^{K}|x^{(j_{t})}-y^{(j_{t})}|^{2}for any1≤jt≤d1\leq j_{t}\leq d. In particular,W2,ℓ∞​(μ,ν)W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}(\mu,\nu)upper bounds the distance between any one-dimensional marginals.

Our analysis for unadjusted overdamped Langevin in[17]proceeds via a coupling argument under theW2,ℓ∞W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}distance. In this paper, we extend that analysis to unadjusted HMC and UL, which are both much more commonly used for complicated, high-dimensional sampling applications than overdamped Langevin.
This
extension introduces significant technical challenges, as the dynamics now evolve on the full phase space(q,p)(q,p)with an auxiliary momentum variable. The coupling framework in[17]requires analyzing the discrete dynamics over multiple steps to bound the accumulation of discretization error.
However, the propagator matrices on the(q,p)(q,p)space exhibit intricate position-momentum coupling, making their multi-step composition difficult to control.
To this end, we introduce a novel matrix polynomial representation of the propagators. Such a representation allows for precise characterization of the multi-step dynamics and enables sharp error bounds under theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}norm.

## 1.2.Literature review

Hamiltonian and underdamped Langevin dynamics have long been foundational to the simulation of physical systems.
HMC was originally introduced in[23]for particle physics, and was later adapted and popularized for modern statistics and machine learning by[42]. Today, it serves as one of the standard samplers in probabilistic programming packages like Stan[13]and PyMC[47]. In the realm of molecular dynamics (MD), unadjusted HMC is closely related to the Andersen
thermostat[4].
Similarly, UL is commonly employed as a robust thermostat[27,3,31]. The BAOAB splitting scheme was introduced in[33]and variants have since been implemented widely in MD software packages[25,1,29,14,44,43,50].

To maintain stability in integration of Hamilton’s ODE (1.1) or the underdamped Langevin SDE (1.2), the step sizehhmust be chosen smaller than the shortest vibrational period among all processes in the system (e.g. about a femtosecond in MD). For adjusted HMC, by contrast,[6]establishes the scalingh∼d−1/4h\sim d^{-1/4}as the requirement for a non-vanishing acceptance probability asd→∞d\to\infty, under a product measure with bounded fourth-order derivatives of the potential.
The same scaling was later shown for an adjusted UL scheme in[45], and also appears in mixing time guarantees for adjusted HMC under warm starts[18]. Recent work[51]shows that unadjusted HMC generates a warm start inO​(d1/4)O(d^{1/4})steps up to logarithmic factors.

Unadjusted algorithms avoid the rejection bottleneck but incur a discretization bias. A rich body of literature has emerged focusing on the non-asymptotic analysis of unadjusted HMC and UL algorithms, establishing bias bounds as the number of iterations goes to infinity.
These analyses rely on various distance metrics and target measure assumptions. The most fundamental setting, detailed in Assumption1.1, requires the potential to be aC2C^{2}function that is strongly convex with Lipschitz gradient. However, because commonly employed integrators possess weak second-order accuracy[34,8], fully demonstrating their accuracy often requires bounding the higher-order derivatives of the potential[39]. Under these frameworks, the theoretical guarantees show power lawsh∼d−ch\sim d^{-c}for some orderc>0c>0depending on the specific assumptions. For unadjusted HMC,[36]requiresh∼d−1/2h\sim d^{-1/2}to bound the Wasserstein distance, while[38]achieves anh∼d−1/4h\sim d^{-1/4}scaling for a high-probability bound under a third-order regularity condition. Furthermore, bias bounds arising from convergence guarantees have been derived in total variation distance (h∼d−3/4h\sim d^{-3/4})[7], Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence (h∼d−3/4h\sim d^{-3/4})[9], and Rényi divergence (h∼d−3/2h\sim d^{-3/2})[9]. For unadjusted UL,[32]establishes the Wasserstein bias for the BAOAB scheme, requiringh∼d−1/2h\sim d^{-1/2}under Assumption1.1, with improved dimensional dependence achieved under stricter smoothness assumptions. Wasserstein bias for different integrators of UL was studied in[20,22,39].[39]also establishes a total variation bias bound through a Wasserstein-total variation regularization property. Under KL divergence,[35]similarly requiresh∼d−1/2h\sim d^{-1/2}. These analyses have also been generalized to non-convex settings using techniques such as reflection coupling[26,48,16]and entropic approaches[41,12].

Recent literature has increasingly unified the theoretical analysis of HMC and UL algorithms. For instance, under the umbrella framework of “generalized HMC” (gHMC),[28]determines the optimal scaling of the friction parameter and the HMC integration time to attain accelerated convergence regarding the condition number for Gaussian targets, while[12]develops KL convergence guarantees for gHMC. Moving beyond traditional fixed-step integrators,[49]introduces the Randomized Midpoint Method (RMM) for UL dynamics. They establish an improved dimension dependenceh∼d−1/3h\sim d^{-1/3}in the Wasserstein distance. Inspired by this mechanism,[8]achieves the samed−1/3d^{-1/3}scaling for unadjusted HMC by employing a stratified Monte Carlo integrator.

Crucially, the aforementioned guarantees quantify the error across all variables of the joint system. In contrast, our work builds upon[17,30]to focus exclusively on controlling the error of low-dimensional marginals, which is a more practical notion of error in high-dimensional applications where the majority of coordinates act as nuisance variables. We previously introduced the term “delocalization of bias” in[17]to describe the phenomenon where theW2W_{2}bias of low-dimensional marginals remains nearly independent of the full system dimension. Our analysis there focused on the unadjusted (overdamped) Langevin algorithm, employing a coupling framework to establish Wasserstein bias bounds for the marginals.[30]investigates this phenomenon under KL divergence using a hierarchical analysis of marginal relative entropies. In[21], a related question is studied for graphical models,
where dimension-independent bounds are established, through Stein’s method, on the marginal error between a
target distribution and its localized approximation.
Prior to our work in[17],[11]and[24]observed that large step sizes remain viable in high dimensions if the observable of interest has a Lipschitz constant that decays with dimension, thereby canceling out the dimensional scaling of the full system’s Wasserstein bias (e.g., an averaged observablef​(x)=1d​∑i=1dϕ​(x(i))f(x)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\phi(x^{(i)})with anℓ2\ell^{2}-Lipschitzϕ\phi). In such regimes, a constant step size is sufficient to achieve a bounded error regardless of dimension. Besides these analysis efforts, there are ample numerical evidence demonstrating the viability of dimension-free step size choices[15,46].

## 1.3.Main results

In[17], we have shown for overdamped Langevin that the delocalization of bias holds for product measures, for Gaussian measures, and for distributions with sparse interactions between variables. We also provided a negative example showing that this effect does not hold universally. In this paper, we establish the delocalization effect for the unadjusted HMC and
UL algorithms under analogous assumptions, namely weak or sparse interactions, or
Gaussian targets. Our analysis is conducted under the following strongly log-concave and log-smooth assumption.

## Assumption 1.1.

Letπ∝exp⁡(−V)\pi\propto\exp(-V)withV∈C2​(ℝd)V\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}). AssumeVVisα\alpha-strongly convex andβ\beta-smooth such thatα​I⪯∇2V​(x)⪯β​I\alpha I\preceq\nabla^{2}V(x)\preceq\beta Ifor anyx∈ℝdx\in\mathbb{R}^{d}, where0<α≤β<∞0<\alpha\leq\beta<\infty.

Throughout, unadjusted HMC uses the leap-frog integrator (1.3) and
unadjusted UL uses the BAOAB scheme (1.4). As a warm-up, we present the delocalization of bias result for Gaussian distributions. Since the BAOAB scheme is unbiased for any Gaussian distribution[34], only unadjusted HMC is discussed in this example. We employ the explicit formula for the biased Gaussian distribution and obtain aW2,ℓ∞W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}bias bound directly. The proof appears in AppendixA.

## Example 1.2(Gaussian distributions).

Considerπ=𝒩​(μ,Σ)\pi=\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)whereμ∈ℝd\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d}andα​I⪯Σ−1⪯β​I\alpha I\preceq\Sigma^{-1}\preceq\beta I. Then, forh<1/βh<1/\sqrt{\beta}, unadjusted HMC, when it converges, has the following bias:(1.7)W2,ℓ∞​(πh,π)=O​(h2​β​log⁡(2​d))=O​(h​log⁡(2​d)).W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}(\pi_{h},\pi)=O(h^{2}\sqrt{\beta\log(2d)})=O(h\sqrt{\log(2d)})\,.

As in the case of overdamped Langevin dynamics[17], the proof of Example1.2offers no insight into which target distributions beyond the Gaussian family evince the delocalization of bias effect for unadjusted HMC or UL. Our main results describe two different sets of structural assumptions onVVunder which this effect appears.
For general distributions satisfying Assumption1.1, our first results address the “weak interactions” setting of[30], made precise below.

## Assumption 1.3(weak interactions).

The Hessian∇2V\nabla^{2}Vadmits the decomposition∇2V=∇2V(D)+∇2V(O)\nabla^{2}V=\nabla^{2}V^{(D)}+\nabla^{2}V^{(O)}where∇2V(D)=diag​(∇2V)\nabla^{2}V^{(D)}=\mathrm{diag}(\nabla^{2}V)is the diagonal part and∇2V(O)\nabla^{2}V^{(O)}is the off-diagonal part. We assume there exists a sufficiently small constantC(O)>0C^{(O)}>0, independent ofα\alpha,β\beta,hh, anddd, such that the the off-diagonal part satisfies|∇2V(O)|ℓ∞≤C(O)​α|\nabla^{2}V^{(O)}|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq C^{(O)}\alpha

uniformly overℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Assumption1.1ensures that every diagonal entry satisfies[∇2V(D)]i​i≥α[\nabla^{2}V^{(D)}]_{ii}\geq\alpha. The interactions defined in Assumption1.3are therefore weak, as the total intensity of interactions between any given variable and all other variables, measured by off-diagonal row sums, is small compared to the independent components on the diagonal.

We now state our main result under weak interactions. The proofs for the two
algorithms are given in AppendixF.

## Theorem 1.4(weak interactions).

Let Assumptions1.1and1.3hold. For either unadjusted HMC withm​h=1/20​βmh=1/\sqrt{20\beta}andC(O)=1/50C^{(O)}=1/50, or unadjusted UL withh≤1−η2​βh\leq\frac{1-\eta}{2\sqrt{\beta}}andC(O)=1/20C^{(O)}=1/20, the resulting invariant distributionπh\pi_{h}satisfies(1.8)W2,ℓ∞​(πh,π)=O​(βα​h​log⁡(2​d)).W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}(\pi_{h},\pi)=O\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}h\sqrt{\log(2d)}\right)\,.

Essentially, the distribution with weak interactions constitutes a perturbation of the product measure. The delocalization effect holds for product measures, since the dynamics are separable for each variable, leading to a dimension-free bias in each coordinate. This effect persists as long as the interactions between variables are sufficiently weak.

The second setting of interest is the case of “sparse interactions” studied in[17], where each variable interacts directly with only a few others. Because the interactions are local, the influence of one variable spreads only gradually through the interaction structure over multiple steps, keeping the marginal bias small. This interaction structure is described by a graphical model, with each variable a node, as illustrated in Figure1. LetGGbe an undirected graph withddnodes, labeled by1≤i≤d1\leq i\leq d, and writei∼ji\sim jwhen nodesiiandjjare connected by an edge. The neighborhood𝖭​(i)\mathsf{N}(i)is the set of nodes
connected toii. Here the neighborhood relationship is symmetric, and without loss of generality, we takei∼ii\sim ifor all1≤i≤d1\leq i\leq d. We quantify the locality of interactions through a growth condition on the
neighborhood sizes. Define recursively𝖭k​(i)={1≤j≤d:∃l∈𝖭k−1​(i),such that​j∼l}\mathsf{N}_{k}(i)=\{1\leq j\leq d:\exists\ l\in\mathsf{N}_{k-1}(i),\text{ such that }j\sim l\}, fork≥2k\geq 2, with𝖭1​(i):=𝖭​(i)\mathsf{N}_{1}(i):=\mathsf{N}(i), and writesk≔max1≤i≤d⁡|𝖭2​k​(i)|s_{k}\coloneqq\max_{1\leq i\leq d}|\mathsf{N}_{2k}(i)|for the relevant
neighborhood cardinality wheresk≥1s_{k}\geq 1by definition. We make the following assumption on the potential.

## Assumption 1.5.

The potential has the form(1.9)V​(x)=∑i=1dVi​(𝒳i),V(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}V_{i}(\mathcal{X}^{i})\,,

where𝒳i={x(j):j∈𝖭​(i)}\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{x^{(j)}:j\in\mathsf{N}(i)\}and eachViV_{i}depends only on the variables in𝒳i\mathcal{X}^{i}. Moreover, the sparsity parametersks_{k}satisfies
the polynomial growth boundsk≤C​(k+1)ns_{k}\leq C(k+1)^{n}for constantsC,n>0C,n>0.

The main result under sparse interactions is as follows. The proofs are given in
AppendixF.Figure 1.Illustration of a potentialV​(x)V(x)with sparse interactions. Adapted from[17].

## Theorem 1.6(sparse interactions).

Let Assumptions1.1and1.5hold. For either unadjusted HMC withm​h=1/20​βmh=1/\sqrt{20\beta}, or unadjusted UL withh≤1−η2​βh\leq\frac{1-\eta}{2\sqrt{\beta}}, the resulting invariant distributionπh\pi_{h}satisfies(1.10)W2,ℓ∞​(πh,π)=h​log⁡(2​d)​(O​(βα​log⁡(2​d)))n2+1.W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}(\pi_{h},\pi)=h\sqrt{\log(2d)}\left(O\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\log(2d)\right)\right)^{\frac{n}{2}+1}\,.

Compared to our results for the unadjusted Langevin algorithm in[17], we have an improvement fromh​log⁡(2​d)\sqrt{h\log(2d)}toh​log⁡(2​d)h\sqrt{\log(2d)}. The new scaling aligns with[32], where the Wasserstein-2 bias of the BAOAB scheme ish​dh\sqrt{d}. It does not show a second order accuracy due to lack of higher order regularity conditions onVV[39,8]. We also note that the step-size conditions for both methods align with existing results. For HMC, the chosen length of the integration time scales with1/β1/\sqrt{\beta}for both the continuous and discrete dynamics[19,11], while we choose the specific constant to optimize the contraction rate in our analysis. For UL, we allow for the same step-size condition as in[32]. Specifically, the condition indicates thath≤12​βh\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\beta}}.
Besides, the relationη=exp⁡(−γ​h)\eta=\exp(-\gamma h)implies the conditionγ>2​β\gamma>2\sqrt{\beta}. In comparison,[40]establishes thatγ>β−α\gamma>\sqrt{\beta}-\sqrt{\alpha}is the sharp condition to guarantee Wasserstein contraction for general distributions, whereas for Gaussian targets, contraction holds for anyγ\gamma. Therefore, in the highly ill-conditioned regime (α≪β\alpha\ll\beta), our friction requirement matches the scaling of this tight condition up to a factor of22. This scaling also aligns with continuous-time dynamics, where the optimal Wasserstein-2 convergence rate ofO​(α/γ)O(\alpha/\gamma)is attained atγ=β+α\gamma=\sqrt{\beta+\alpha}, a value that is asymptotically equivalent toβ\sqrt{\beta}whenα≪β\alpha\ll\beta.
Notably, the step-size condition accommodates the entire high friction regime, and ensures that the bias bounds remain valid in the overdamped limitγ→∞\gamma\to\infty, where the BAOAB scheme reduces to the Leimkuhler-Matthews (LM) integrator, a method for the overdamped dynamics that achieves weak second-order accuracy[33]. Our results thus also imply the delocalization of bias effect for the LM scheme.

The key technical challenge of extending our analysis in[17]is that the propagator matrices of HMC and UL are more involved. The coupling argument, a common device for obtaining Wasserstein bounds, relies on the propagator of the dynamics to induce contraction and to control the accumulation of discretization error. The lack of one-step contraction in theW2,ℓ∞W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}metric necessitated the use of a multi-step coupling argument in[17], where the sparsity of the potential was leveraged to control the accumulated errors over these steps.
In this paper, we employ a novel matrix polynomial framework that applies to both HMC and UL, to characterize the multi-step propagator matrices
on the joint(q,p)(q,p)space. The framework takes a global-in-time view, giving the full multi-step propagator
at once as a polynomial of the Hessians along the trajectory, with coefficients we
can characterize and bound. On the joint(q,p)(q,p)space, position and momentum differences mix as the trajectory evolves, so the position-difference propagator is not a simple product of one-step maps but a structured polynomial pairing the two. The matrix-polynomial framework makes this structure explicit. The polynomial’s coefficients control both the contraction and the error accumulation in theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}norm. Under sparse interactions, in particular, theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}norm of the multi-step propagator is magnified over itsℓ2\ell^{2}norm by only a factor governed by the potential’s sparsity. In the easier weak interaction regime, the propagators are nearly diagonal, so contraction
follows from a more elementary, near-single-step argument. For HMC this
contraction is still read off from the multi-step polynomial, since one outer loop
comprises several integration steps, whereas for UL it follows directly from the
splitting structure of the scheme without invoking the polynomial.

## 1.4.Organization of this paper

In Section2, we present our analysis framework based on the coupling argument and introduce the notation for the dynamics. Section3introduces a matrix polynomial framework to characterize the propagator matrices, and Section4establishes bounds for these propagators. Discretization error estimates are provided in Section5. We conclude the paper in Section6. All proofs are deferred to the appendices.

## 1.5.Notation

For a random vectorXX, we define|X|2,ℓ∞=(𝔼​[|X|ℓ∞2])1/2|X|_{2,\ell^{\infty}}=(\mathbb{E}[|X|_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2}])^{1/2}. We writeA=O​(B)A=O(B)orA≲BA\lesssim Bto denote that there exists a constantCCindependent ofα,β,h,d\alpha,\beta,h,dsuch thatA≤C​BA\leq CB.
We use|⋅|ℓ∞|\cdot|_{\ell^{\infty}}and|⋅|ℓ2|\cdot|_{\ell^{2}}to represent theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}andℓ2\ell^{2}norms for vectors and matrices. When applied to matrices, they stand for the corresponding operator norms. We use⪯\preceqfor the Loewner order such that ifM⪯NM\preceq NwhereM,NM,Nare symmetric
matrices, then the matrixN−MN-Mis positive semi-definite. We use⌊x⌋\lfloor x\rfloorand⌈x⌉\lceil x\rceilto denote the greatest integer less than or equal toxx, and the least integer greater than or equal toxx, respectively.

## 2.Sketch of Techniques

In this section, we outline our framework of the coupling argument and identify the propagator matrices as the key components for analysis.

## 2.1.Wasserstein bias

Our proof utilizes a coupling argument to establish both the Wasserstein convergence rate and the accumulated discretization error across iterations. These results then yield the final Wasserstein bias bounds.
The following proposition outlines the Wasserstein convergence for HMC when the potential describes weak interactions.

## Proposition 2.1(HMC, weak interactions).

Suppose Assumptions1.1and1.3hold withC(O)=1/50C^{(O)}=1/50. Letρk\rho_{k}be the marginal distribution ofXkX_{k}, thekk-th step in the HMC chain. Form​h=1/20​βmh=1/\sqrt{20\beta}, we have(2.1)W2,ℓ∞​(ρk+1,π)≤(1−α400​β)​W2,ℓ∞​(ρk,π)+O​(h​log⁡(2​d)).W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}(\rho_{k+1},\pi)\leq\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{400\beta}\right)W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}(\rho_{k},\pi)+O(h\sqrt{\log(2d)})\,.

By takingk→∞k\to\infty, the iterative inequality (2.1) establishes the final bias bound in Theorem1.4. The inequality consists of two components. The first characterizes the Wasserstein contraction rate of the HMC chain, while the second bounds the discretization error relative to an ideal HMC process that preservesπ\piexactly. The detailed proof is in AppendixF. For the case of sparse interactions, we instead rely on a multi-step recursive inequality.

## Proposition 2.2(HMC, sparse interactions).

Suppose Assumptions1.1and1.5hold, and setri≔⌈i​h​β​e+log⁡dlog⁡(5/3)⌉r_{i}\coloneqq\lceil ih\sqrt{\beta}e+\frac{\log\sqrt{d}}{\log(5/3)}\rceil, a
neighborhood threshold chosen to control theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}norm of the
propagators as in Section4.3.
Whenm​h=1/20​βmh=1/\sqrt{20\beta},
we have(2.2)W2,ℓ∞​(ρk+N,π)≤(1−α200​β)N​d​W2,ℓ∞​(ρk,π)+O​((∑i=0N​m−1sri)​β​h2​log⁡(2​d)).W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}(\rho_{k+N},\pi)\leq\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{200\beta}\right)^{N}\sqrt{d}W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}(\rho_{k},\pi)+O\left(\left(\sum_{i=0}^{Nm-1}\sqrt{s_{r_{i}}}\right)\sqrt{\beta}h^{2}\sqrt{\log(2d)}\right)\,.

See AppendixFfor the detailed proof. When deriving a bias bound, to preserve contraction in the first term, we takeN∼(β/α)​log⁡dN\sim(\beta/\alpha)\log d, which necessitates a multi-step coupling. The discretization error remains controlled due to the sparsity of the Hessians, which manifests as thesri\sqrt{s_{r_{i}}}terms in the bound. By takingk→∞k\to\infty, (2.2) results in the final bias bound in Theorem1.6.

We now lay out the coupling framework underlying Propositions2.1and2.2. The framework is common to HMC and UL. Once a contraction is established, over one step or several, the total bias is a sum of single-step discretization errors, each propagated forward under the discrete-time scheme. The object governing this propagation, which is thepropagator matrixacting on the difference between two paths, is therefore central to the argument. Working with the discrete-time scheme is itself a deliberate choice, as the existingℓ2\ell^{2}analyses in continuous time[19,37]exploit the inner-product structure, but theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}norm is less compatible with continuous-time differentiation. For HMC, the propagator matrices contract in theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}norm in one outer loop under weak interactions, while theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}contraction emerges over multiple outer loops under sparse interactions. Moreover, the same class of matrices controls how the discretization error propagates across inner steps and subsequent outer loops.

The UL analysis follows a similar template, but its iterative inequalities are more involved due to a weighted norm and the absence of
momentum refreshment. We therefore
present the framework for both algorithms but give the iterative inequalities only
for HMC.

## 2.3.UL

The framework for UL follows that for HMC, with two modifications. First, the
dynamics evolve without momentum refreshment, so we do not project onto the
position between steps. Second, since contraction in theℓ2\ell^{2}norm
requires a weighted metric[39,32], we adopt a weightedℓ∞\ell^{\infty}norm throughout.

steps. Since we only need the asymptotic bias in the position variable, one

the position coordinates, whose marginal is non-Markovian across intermediate

state through to the final stepkkrather than step by step.

omitted for brevity. Each term is the error over at mostl~\tilde{l}steps,
propagated through the subsequent BAOAB steps. Characterizing these propagator
matrices for the evolution of the difference between two paths is therefore central to the accumulated discretization-error analysis.

## 3.A Matrix Polynomial Framework for Propagator Analysis

As shown in the previous section, the propagator matrices are fundamental to characterizing both the contraction rates and the discretization error under theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}norm. In the absence of continuous-time differentiation, we track the evolution of path differences by introducing a framework that expresses these propagators asmatrix polynomials. The underlying polynomial coefficients then facilitate the derivation of the requiredℓ∞\ell^{\infty}bounds. We illustrate this structure with a Gaussian distribution.

## 3.1.Propagators: Gaussian examples

The propagators of the leap-frog scheme are closely related to Chebyshev polynomials, as illustrated by the following proposition.

## Proposition 3.1.

Forπ=𝒩​(0,Σ)\pi=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma), afterkkleap-frog steps, the difference between the position componentsxk=Π1​𝖴hmc,hk​(x0,p0)x_{k}=\mathsf{\Pi}_{1}\mathsf{U}_{\text{hmc},h}^{k}(x_{0},p_{0})andyk=Π1​𝖴hmc,hk​(y0,p0′)y_{k}=\mathsf{\Pi}_{1}\mathsf{U}_{\text{hmc},h}^{k}(y_{0},p^{\prime}_{0})satisfies(3.1)xk−yk=Tk​(I−h22​Σ−1)​(x0−y0)+h​Uk−1​(I−h22​Σ−1)​(p0−p0′),x_{k}-y_{k}=T_{k}(I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}\Sigma^{-1})(x_{0}-y_{0})+hU_{k-1}(I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}\Sigma^{-1})(p_{0}-p^{\prime}_{0})\,,

whereTkT_{k}is thekk-th Chebyshev polynomial of the first kind, andUkU_{k}is thekk-th Chebyshev polynomial of the second kind. We adopt the conventionU−1=0U_{-1}=0, which is consistent with the standard three-term recurrence relationUk+1​(x)=2​x​Uk​(x)−Uk−1​(x)U_{k+1}(x)=2xU_{k}(x)-U_{k-1}(x)fork≥0k\geq 0when initializing the sequence atU0​(x)=1U_{0}(x)=1.

## Proof sketch.

Writing down the iteration (1.3) in two consecutive steps and eliminating the momentum
difference yields, forΔ​qk≔xk−yk\Delta q_{k}\coloneqq x_{k}-y_{k}, the three-term recurrence(3.2)Δ​qk+1=(2​I−h2​Σ−1)​Δ​qk−Δ​qk−1,k≥1.\Delta q_{k+1}=(2I-h^{2}\Sigma^{-1})\Delta q_{k}-\Delta q_{k-1}\,,\quad k\geq 1.

Deriving the corresponding recurrence for the propagator matrices reveals the Chebyshev polynomials.
∎

This is a special case of Proposition3.5for which we provide the complete proof. It is natural forΔ​qk\Delta q_{k}to satisfy a three-term recurrence, as the leap-frog scheme is a discretization of a second-order ordinary differential equation in the position variable. In fact, the position-only iteration (3.2) is the Störmer form of the leap-frog scheme[31]. The relation to the Chebyshev polynomials is also well-motivated. For instance, in a one-dimensional system with the quadratic potentialV​(q)=12​q2V(q)=\frac{1}{2}q^{2}, the exact solution isq​(t)=q​(0)​cos⁡(t)+p​(0)​sin⁡(t)q(t)=q(0)\cos(t)+p(0)\sin(t). The Chebyshev polynomials provide the discrete analogue to these trigonometric solutions via the identitiesTk​(cos⁡θ)=cos⁡(k​θ)T_{k}(\cos\theta)=\cos(k\theta)andUk−1​(cos⁡θ)=sin⁡(k​θ)/sin⁡θU_{k-1}(\cos\theta)=\sin(k\theta)/\sin\theta.

With momentum refreshment between outer loops, multiplying their matrix polynomials
gives the following HMC propagator across multiple loops.

## Proposition 3.2.

Forπ=𝒩​(0,Σ)\pi=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma), letxN​m+kx_{Nm+k}andyN​m+ky_{Nm+k}denote the position components after initialkkleap-frog steps, followed byNNfull HMC outer loops (each consisting ofmmsteps). Specifically, letxN​m+k≔Φh,ξN−1m​⋯​Φh,ξ0m​Π1​𝖴hmc,hk​(x0,p0)x_{Nm+k}\coloneqq\mathsf{\Phi}_{h,\xi_{N-1}}^{m}\cdots\mathsf{\Phi}_{h,\xi_{0}}^{m}\mathsf{\Pi}_{1}\mathsf{U}_{\text{hmc},h}^{k}(x_{0},p_{0})andyN​m+k≔Φh,ξN−1m​⋯​Φh,ξ0m​Π1​𝖴hmc,hk​(y0,p0′)y_{Nm+k}\coloneqq\mathsf{\Phi}_{h,\xi_{N-1}}^{m}\cdots\mathsf{\Phi}_{h,\xi_{0}}^{m}\mathsf{\Pi}_{1}\mathsf{U}_{\text{hmc},h}^{k}(y_{0},p^{\prime}_{0}). The differenceΔ​qN​m+k≔xN​m+k−yN​m+k\Delta q_{Nm+k}\coloneqq x_{Nm+k}-y_{Nm+k}satisfies(3.3)Δ​qN​m+k\displaystyle\Delta q_{Nm+k}=(Tm​(I−h22​Σ−1))N\displaystyle=\left(T_{m}(I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}\Sigma^{-1})\right)^{N}⋅(Tk​(I−h22​Σ−1)​(x0−y0)+h​Uk−1​(I−h22​Σ−1)​(p0−p0′)).\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\left(T_{k}(I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}\Sigma^{-1})(x_{0}-y_{0})+hU_{k-1}(I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}\Sigma^{-1})(p_{0}-p^{\prime}_{0})\right)\,.

Here,xN​m+kx_{Nm+k}andyN​m+ky_{Nm+k}are coupled by utilizing identical momentum refreshmentsξ0,…,ξN−1\xi_{0},\ldots,\xi_{N-1}at each outer loop.

The UL dynamics are closely related to the Hamiltonian dynamics but crucially involve a friction term. The friction induces a modification to the polynomial structure. Specifically, the resulting three-term recurrence for the BAOAB scheme incorporates damped coefficients, which recovers the leap-frog coefficients by takingγ=0\gamma=0.

## Proposition 3.3.

Forπ=𝒩​(0,Σ)\pi=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma), letxkx_{k}andyky_{k}denote the position components afterkkBAOAB steps. Specifically, letxk≔Π1​𝖴baoab,hξk−1​⋯​𝖴baoab,hξ0​(x0,p0)x_{k}\coloneqq\mathsf{\Pi}_{1}\mathsf{U}_{\text{baoab},h}^{\xi_{k-1}}\cdots\mathsf{U}_{\text{baoab},h}^{\xi_{0}}(x_{0},p_{0})andyk≔Π1​𝖴baoab,hξk−1​⋯​𝖴baoab,hξ0​(y0,p0′)y_{k}\coloneqq\mathsf{\Pi}_{1}\mathsf{U}_{\text{baoab},h}^{\xi_{k-1}}\cdots\mathsf{U}_{\text{baoab},h}^{\xi_{0}}(y_{0},p^{\prime}_{0}). The differenceΔ​qk≔xk−yk\Delta q_{k}\coloneqq x_{k}-y_{k}then satisfies(3.4)Δ​qk+1=(1+η)​(I−h22​Σ−1)​Δ​qk−η​Δ​qk−1,k≥1.\Delta q_{k+1}=(1+\eta)(I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}\Sigma^{-1})\Delta q_{k}-\eta\Delta q_{k-1}\,,\quad k\geq 1.

## 3.2.Propagators: general cases

Inspired by the Gaussian case, we establish that the propagator matrices can be represented as matrix polynomials, whose coefficients can be accurately characterized. However, the Hessian varies along the trajectory, and the
resulting matrices do not commute. The polynomial must therefore be generalized to
one in several ordered, non-commuting arguments, each monomial being a product
taken in a fixed order. We view the general propagator as a “path-dependent” polynomial, where the recurrence remains identical to the Gaussian case but the arguments are updated in order at each step. We now define these polynomials.

## Definition 3.4(Multivariate Damped Chebyshev Polynomials).

For a sequence of matricesMi∈ℝd×dM_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}, we define the
multivariate damped Chebyshev polynomial of the first kind,T~kη​(M1,…,Mk)\tilde{T}_{k}^{\eta}(M_{1},\dots,M_{k}), via the three-term recurrence(3.5)T~k+1η​(M1,…,Mk+1)=(1+η)​Mk+1​T~kη​(M1,…,Mk)−η​T~k−1η​(M1,…,Mk−1)\tilde{T}_{k+1}^{\eta}(M_{1},\dots,M_{k+1})=(1+\eta)\,M_{k+1}\tilde{T}_{k}^{\eta}(M_{1},\dots,M_{k})-\eta\,\tilde{T}_{k-1}^{\eta}(M_{1},\dots,M_{k-1})

fork≥1k\geq 1, with the initial conditionsT~0η≡I\tilde{T}_{0}^{\eta}\equiv IandT~1η​(M1)=1+η2​M1+1−η2​I\tilde{T}_{1}^{\eta}(M_{1})=\tfrac{1+\eta}{2}M_{1}+\tfrac{1-\eta}{2}I. Note that the new
argumentMk+1M_{k+1}enters on the left.
The multivariate damped Chebyshev polynomial of
the second kind,U~kη​(M1,…,Mk)\tilde{U}_{k}^{\eta}(M_{1},\dots,M_{k}), is defined by the same
recurrence(3.6)U~k+1η​(M1,…,Mk+1)=(1+η)​Mk+1​U~kη​(M1,…,Mk)−η​U~k−1η​(M1,…,Mk−1)\tilde{U}_{k+1}^{\eta}(M_{1},\dots,M_{k+1})=(1+\eta)M_{k+1}\tilde{U}_{k}^{\eta}(M_{1},\dots,M_{k})-\eta\tilde{U}_{k-1}^{\eta}(M_{1},\dots,M_{k-1})

fork≥0k\geq 0, with different initial conditionsU~−1η≡0\tilde{U}_{-1}^{\eta}\equiv 0,U~0η≡1+η2​I\tilde{U}_{0}^{\eta}\equiv\tfrac{1+\eta}{2}I. For notation brevity, we also adopt the sequence notationT~kη​({Mi}i=1k)\tilde{T}_{k}^{\eta}(\{M_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k})andU~kη​({Mi}i=1k)\tilde{U}_{k}^{\eta}(\{M_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}).

We note that whenη=1\eta=1, which corresponds to the frictionless Hamiltonian regimeγ=0\gamma=0, setting allMi≔x​IM_{i}\coloneqq xIwith a scalarxxgivesT~kη​(x​I,…,x​I)=Tk​(x)​I\tilde{T}_{k}^{\eta}(xI,\dots,xI)=T_{k}(x)IandU~kη​(x​I,…,x​I)=Uk​(x)​I\tilde{U}_{k}^{\eta}(xI,\dots,xI)=U_{k}(x)I, recovering the classical Chebyshev
polynomialsTkT_{k}andUkU_{k}. We also note that, even for scalar arguments, our polynomials differ from the
conventional multivariate Chebyshev polynomials onℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, which are built
from tensor products of one-dimensional polynomials rather than a recurrence in
ordered arguments. In the following proposition, we show that the multivariate damped Chebyshev polynomials govern the general propagator matrices in the leap-frog and BAOAB schemes. In particular, it includes the Gaussian propositions stated above. We present its proof in AppendixB.

## 4.Bounds for Propagators

In this section, we presentℓ2\ell^{2}andℓ∞\ell^{\infty}bounds for propagators, instantiating the technical framework sketched in Section2. While theℓ2\ell^{2}bounds are obtained by adapting existing techniques to our setting, theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}bounds represent our primary analytical contribution. These estimates hinge on the structural properties of the multivariate damped Chebyshev polynomials, which allow us to bridge fromℓ2\ell^{2}stability toℓ∞\ell^{\infty}guarantees under the assumption of weak or sparse interactions.

## 4.1.ℓ2\ell^{2}bounds

The relevantℓ2\ell^{2}bounds for HMC are as follows.

## Proposition 4.1(ℓ2\ell^{2}bounds for HMC propagators).

Suppose Assumption1.1hold. IfHi=∫∇2V​d​νiH_{i}=\int\nabla^{2}V{\rm d}\nu_{i}for a probability measureνi\nu_{i}onℝd\mathbb{R}^{d},
we have the followingℓ2\ell^{2}bounds for the leap-frog propagatorsT~k\tilde{T}_{k}andU~k−1\tilde{U}_{k-1}providedk​h≤1/20​βkh\leq 1/\sqrt{20\beta}:(4.1)|T~k1​({I−h22​Hi}i=0k−1)|ℓ2≤1−α10​(k​h)2,|h​U~k−11​({I−h22​Hi}i=1k−1)|ℓ2≤25​β.|\tilde{T}_{k}^{1}(\{I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}H_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k-1})|_{\ell^{2}}\leq 1-\frac{\alpha}{10}(kh)^{2}\,,\quad|h\tilde{U}_{k-1}^{1}(\{I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}H_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k-1})|_{\ell^{2}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{5\beta}}\,.

The proof is provided in AppendixC. Combined with Proposition3.5, Proposition4.1states that the leap-frog scheme induces a contraction in the position components, provided the trajectories are initialized with identical momentum and the total integration time remains within a specific threshold scaled as1/β1/\sqrt{\beta}.
The momentum difference may expand, though this growth remains well-controlled by a magnitude scaled as1/β1/\sqrt{\beta}. These scalings are consistent with the Gaussian setting under the continuous-time dynamics within the time range. We adapt the proof of an existing discrete-time contraction result[11, Lemma 19]to obtain the bound forT~k1\tilde{T}_{k}^{1}. Applying a similar framework for the momentum components, we establish theU~k1\tilde{U}_{k}^{1}bound.

For UL, contraction is induced in the weighted norms of

propagator representation. the unweighted difference, parametrized by an integrated HessianH0H_{0}.

After one step, we have(4.3)Π1​(z1−z1′)=[I0]​W−1​Mw​(H0)​W​(z0−z0′).\mathsf{\Pi}_{1}(z_{1}-z^{\prime}_{1})=\begin{bmatrix}I&0\end{bmatrix}W^{-1}M^{w}(H_{0})\,W(z_{0}-z^{\prime}_{0})\,.

## Proposition 4.2(ℓ2\ell^{2}bounds for UL propagators).

SupposeHiH_{i}satisfies the same assumptions as in Proposition4.1. For a step sizeh≤1−η2​βh\leq\frac{1-\eta}{2\sqrt{\beta}}, and lettingc​(h)=α​h24​(1−η)c(h)=\frac{\alpha h^{2}}{4(1-\eta)}, the full-space weighted BAOAB propagator satisfies(4.4)|Mbaoab,kw|ℓ2≤7​(1−c​(h))k−12,|M_{\text{baoab},k}^{w}|_{\ell^{2}}\leq 7(1-c(h))^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\,,

## 4.2.ℓ∞\ell^{\infty}bounds under weak interactions

The weak interactions defined in Assumption1.3indicate that∇2V\nabla^{2}Vis a small perturbation of a diagonal matrix. The resulting
propagators therefore haveℓ∞\ell^{\infty}norms close to theirℓ2\ell^{2}norms,
since the two norms coincide for a diagonal matrix, and a contraction is preserved inℓ∞\ell^{\infty}whenever the interactions are weak. For HMC, since one outer loop ismmleap-frog steps, the relevant object is the multi-step propagatorT~k1\tilde{T}_{k}^{1}.

## Proposition 4.3(ℓ∞\ell^{\infty}bounds under weak interactions, HMC).

Suppose Assumptions1.1and1.3hold with the weak interaction parameterC(O)=1/50C^{(O)}=1/50. Let the integrated HessiansHiH_{i}be defined as in Proposition4.1. Fork​h≤1/20​βkh\leq 1/\sqrt{20\beta}, we have(4.8)|T~k1​({I−h22​Hi}i=0k−1)|ℓ∞≤1−α10​(k​h)2+k​h​α100​β,|h​U~k−11​({I−h22​Hi}i=1k−1)|ℓ∞≤32​β.|\tilde{T}_{k}^{1}(\{I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}H_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k-1})|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq 1-\frac{\alpha}{10}(kh)^{2}+\frac{kh\alpha}{100\sqrt{\beta}}\,,\quad|h\tilde{U}_{k-1}^{1}(\{I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}H_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k-1})|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq\frac{3}{2\sqrt{\beta}}\,.

In particular, fork​h=120​βkh=\frac{1}{\sqrt{20\beta}}, we have the contraction|T~k1​({I−h22​Hi}i=0k−1)|ℓ∞≤1−α400​β|\tilde{T}_{k}^{1}(\{I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}H_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k-1})|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq 1-\frac{\alpha}{400\beta}.

The proof can be found in AppendixC.

For UL, by contrast, the contraction is obtained at the level of a single step
rather than through a multi-step polynomial. We decompose the full𝖡𝖠𝖮𝖠𝖡\mathsf{B}\mathsf{A}\mathsf{O}\mathsf{A}\mathsf{B}operator into𝖡𝖠𝖮\mathsf{B}\mathsf{A}\mathsf{O},𝖠𝖡𝖠𝖮\mathsf{A}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{A}\mathsf{O}, and𝖠𝖡\mathsf{A}\mathsf{B}, and establish a one-step contraction for the𝖠𝖡𝖠𝖮\mathsf{A}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{A}\mathsf{O}operator.
As in the general
construction (4.2),Mabaow​(H0)M_{\text{abao}}^{w}(H_{0})is the weighted one-step propagator of the𝖴abao,hξ\mathsf{U}_{\text{abao},h}^{\xi}map, satisfyingW​(𝖴abao,hξ​(z0)−𝖴abao,hξ​(z0′))=Mabaow​(H0)​W​(z0−z0′)W(\mathsf{U}_{\text{abao},h}^{\xi}(z_{0})-\mathsf{U}_{\text{abao},h}^{\xi}(z^{\prime}_{0}))=M_{\text{abao}}^{w}(H_{0})\,W(z_{0}-z^{\prime}_{0})for statesz0=(x0,p0)z_{0}=(x_{0},p_{0}),z0′=(y0,p0′)z^{\prime}_{0}=(y_{0},p^{\prime}_{0})coupled through the same
O-step noise.
HereH0=∫01∇2V​(τ​(x0+h2​p0)+(1−τ)​(y0+h2​p0′))​dτH_{0}=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}V\big(\tau(x_{0}+\tfrac{h}{2}p_{0})+(1-\tau)(y_{0}+\tfrac{h}{2}p^{\prime}_{0})\big)\,{\rm d}\tau, and(4.9)Mabaow​(H0)=[I−h22​H0−η1−η​h2​H0h3​14+η(1−η)21/β−h2/(1−η)2​H0−η​h​1β−h2(1−η)2​H0η​(I+(−12+11−η)​h2​H0)].M_{\text{abao}}^{w}(H_{0})=\begin{bmatrix}I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}H_{0}-\frac{\eta}{1-\eta}h^{2}H_{0}&h^{3}\frac{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\eta}{(1-\eta)^{2}}}{\sqrt{1/\beta-h^{2}/(1-\eta)^{2}}}H_{0}\\[4.0pt]
-\eta h\sqrt{\frac{1}{\beta}-\frac{h^{2}}{(1-\eta)^{2}}}H_{0}&\eta\big(I+(-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{1-\eta})h^{2}H_{0}\big)\end{bmatrix}\,.

We work with this full phase-space propagator rather than its position
projection because the𝖠𝖡𝖠𝖮\mathsf{A}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{A}\mathsf{O}operator drives contraction in the joint
space over several iterations before the position marginal is extracted. The
mapsMbaowM_{\text{bao}}^{w}andMabwM_{\text{ab}}^{w}are defined analogously by their one-step
propagation of the weighted state differences, though the state combination over
whichH0H_{0}integrates may differ between schemes.

## Proposition 4.4(ℓ∞\ell^{\infty}bounds under weak interactions, UL).

Suppose Assumptions1.1and1.3hold withC(O)=1/20C^{(O)}=1/20. Let the integrated HessianH0H_{0}be defined as in Proposition4.1. Forh≤1−η2​βh\leq\frac{1-\eta}{2\sqrt{\beta}}and recallingc​(h)=α​h24​(1−η)c(h)=\frac{\alpha h^{2}}{4(1-\eta)}from Proposition4.2, we have(4.10)|Mabaow​(H0)|ℓ∞≤1−12​c​(h),|Mbaow​(H0)|ℓ∞≤3,|Mabw​(H0)|ℓ∞≤2.|M_{\text{abao}}^{w}(H_{0})|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq 1-\frac{1}{2}c(h)\,,\quad|M_{\text{bao}}^{w}(H_{0})|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq 3\,,\quad|M_{\text{ab}}^{w}(H_{0})|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq 2\,.

These bounds are theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}analogues of theℓ2\ell^{2}ingredients used to
prove BAOAB contraction in[32]. See AppendixDfor its proof.

## 4.3.ℓ∞\ell^{\infty}bounds under sparse interactions

We now turn to the propagator bounds under sparse interactions.
Recall from
Assumption1.5that each termViV_{i}depends only on the
coordinates in the neighborhood𝖭​(i)\mathsf{N}(i). A single Hessian∇2V\nabla^{2}Vtherefore has a nonzero(i,j)(i,j)entry only when some
termVlV_{l}involves bothiiandjj, that is,i,j∈𝖭​(l)i,j\in\mathsf{N}(l), soiiandjjare within two edges in the interaction graph. More generally, LemmaC.2in AppendixCshows that a product ofrrHessians, the basic building block of the matrix
polynomials, has its(i,j)(i,j)entry supported onj∈𝖭2​r​(i)j\in\mathsf{N}_{2r}(i), with at mostsrs_{r}nonzero entries per
row.

This locality is what yields theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}bounds. To bound a row of a matrix
polynomial, we fix a degree thresholdrrand split the entries by neighborhood.
Forj∈𝖭2​r​(i)j\in\mathsf{N}_{2r}(i), there are at mostsrs_{r}such indices, and a
Cauchy–Schwarz estimate over them bounds their contribution bysr\sqrt{s_{r}}times theℓ2\ell^{2}norm. Forj∉𝖭2​r​(i)j\notin\mathsf{N}_{2r}(i),
the entry only arises from
products of degree aboverr, which carry high powers of the step size and
decay geometrically. Boundingsrs_{r}together with this decay converts theℓ2\ell^{2}propagator estimates intoℓ∞\ell^{\infty}ones and controls error
accumulation. The following proposition is proved in AppendixC.

## Proposition 4.5(ℓ∞\ell^{\infty}bounds under sparse interactions, HMC).

Suppose Assumptions1.1and1.5hold. Let the integrated HessiansHiH_{i}be defined as in Proposition4.1. Takeri=⌈i​h​β​e+log⁡dlog⁡(5/3)⌉r_{i}=\lceil ih\sqrt{\beta}e+\frac{\log\sqrt{d}}{\log(5/3)}\rceil. Form​h≤1/20​βmh\leq 1/\sqrt{20\beta}, integersN≥0N\geq 0andk<mk<m, we have(4.11)|(∏j∈{k+i​m}i=0N−1⟵T~m1​({I−h22​Hj+l}l=0m−1))​T~k1​({I−h22​Hi}i=0k−1)|ℓ∞≤4​srN​m+k,\Big|\Big(\prod_{j\in\{k+im\}_{i=0}^{N-1}}^{\longleftarrow}\tilde{T}_{m}^{1}(\{I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}H_{j+l}\}_{l=0}^{m-1})\Big)\tilde{T}_{k}^{1}(\{I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}H_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k-1})\Big|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq 4\sqrt{s_{r_{Nm+k}}}\,,(4.12)|(∏j∈{k+i​m}i=0N−1⟵T~m1​({I−h22​Hj+l}l=0m−1))​h​U~k−11​({I−h22​Hi}i=1k−1)|ℓ∞≤4β​srN​m+k.\Big|\Big(\prod_{j\in\{k+im\}_{i=0}^{N-1}}^{\longleftarrow}\tilde{T}_{m}^{1}(\{I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}H_{j+l}\}_{l=0}^{m-1})\Big)h\tilde{U}_{k-1}^{1}(\{I-\frac{h^{2}}{2}H_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k-1})\Big|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq\frac{4}{\sqrt{\beta}}\sqrt{s_{r_{Nm+k}}}\,.

For the BAOAB scheme, the weighted matrices relevant to the position projection have the followingℓ∞\ell^{\infty}bounds. The proof is provided in AppendixD.

## 5.Discretization Error and Sampling Bias

Besides the propagator bounds of Section4, the other critical component of the coupling framework in Section2is the discretization error. In this section
we bound it for HMC and UL, then combine it with the
propagator bounds
to obtain the sampling bias.

## 5.1.Discretization error in HMC

To analyze the position and momentum errors separately, we split each solution map
into its position and momentum components, writing𝖴hmct=(𝖰t,𝖯t)\mathsf{U}_{\text{hmc}}^{t}=(\mathsf{Q}^{t},\mathsf{P}^{t})for
the continuous Hamiltonian dynamics (1.1), and𝖴hmc,hm=(𝖰hm,𝖯hm)\mathsf{U}_{\text{hmc},h}^{m}=(\mathsf{Q}_{h}^{m},\mathsf{P}_{h}^{m})formmleap-frog
steps (1.3). The single-step discretization error then splits as(5.1)(𝖴hmc,h1−𝖴hmch)​(q∗,p∗)=((𝖰h1−𝖰h)​(q∗,p∗),(𝖯h1−𝖯h)​(q∗,p∗)),(\mathsf{U}_{\text{hmc},h}^{1}-\mathsf{U}_{\text{hmc}}^{h})(q^{*},p^{*})=\big((\mathsf{Q}_{h}^{1}-\mathsf{Q}^{h})(q^{*},p^{*}),\ (\mathsf{P}_{h}^{1}-\mathsf{P}^{h})(q^{*},p^{*})\big)\,,

which is the common building block of
(2.6) and
(2.7). The
following proposition bounds each component. We provide the proof to a generalized version in
AppendixE.

## Proposition 5.1.

Suppose for any probability measuresν1,ν2\nu_{1},\nu_{2}onℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, it holds that|∫∇2V​d​ν1|ℓ∞<C3|\int\nabla^{2}V{\rm d}\nu_{1}|_{\ell^{\infty}}<C_{3}and|(∫∇2V​d​ν1)​(∫∇2V​d​ν2)|ℓ∞<C4|(\int\nabla^{2}V{\rm d}\nu_{1})(\int\nabla^{2}V{\rm d}\nu_{2})|_{\ell^{\infty}}<C_{4}for constantsC3,C4>0C_{3},C_{4}>0that may depend onβ\beta. Then forh≤1/20​βh\leq 1/\sqrt{20\beta}and(q∗,p∗)∼π⊗𝒩​(0,I)(q^{*},p^{*})\sim\pi\otimes\mathcal{N}(0,I), we have(5.2)|𝖰h1​(q∗,p∗)−𝖰h​(q∗,p∗)|2,ℓ∞≤12​h3​C3​log⁡(2​d),|\mathsf{Q}_{h}^{1}(q^{*},p^{*})-\mathsf{Q}^{h}(q^{*},p^{*})|_{2,\ell^{\infty}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}h^{3}C_{3}\sqrt{\log(2d)}\,,(5.3)|𝖯h1​(q∗,p∗)−𝖯h​(q∗,p∗)|2,ℓ∞≤2​h2​C3​log⁡(2​d)+12​5​h4​C4​log⁡(2​d).|\mathsf{P}_{h}^{1}(q^{*},p^{*})-\mathsf{P}^{h}(q^{*},p^{*})|_{2,\ell^{\infty}}\leq 2h^{2}C_{3}\sqrt{\log(2d)}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{5}}h^{4}C_{4}\sqrt{\log(2d)}\,.

## 5.2.Discretization error in UL

For the BAOAB integrator we adapt the framework of[32], comparing the BAOAB chain with an HOH chain for an accurate discretization error estimate even in the large-γ\gammalimit.
Our analysis differs mainly in measuring the error in theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}norm
rather thanℓ2\ell^{2}.
The key component of
(2.13) is(𝖴baoab,hl−𝖴hoh,hl)​(q∗,p∗)(\mathsf{U}_{\text{baoab},h}^{l}-\mathsf{U}_{\text{hoh},h}^{l})(q^{*},p^{*}). Runllsteps of
each scheme from thesameinitial state(q∗,p∗)(q^{*},p^{*})under thesameO-step noiseξ0,…,ξl−1\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{l-1}:(5.4)(xi,pi)=𝖴hoh,hξi−1​(xi−1,pi−1),(yi,pi′)=𝖴baoab,hξi−1​(yi−1,pi−1′),(x_{i},p_{i})=\mathsf{U}_{\text{hoh},h}^{\xi_{i-1}}(x_{i-1},p_{i-1})\,,\qquad(y_{i},p^{\prime}_{i})=\mathsf{U}_{\text{baoab},h}^{\xi_{i-1}}(y_{i-1},p^{\prime}_{i-1})\,,

with(x0,p0)=(y0,p0′)=(q∗,p∗)(x_{0},p_{0})=(y_{0},p_{0}^{\prime})=(q^{*},p^{*}). WritingΔqi=xi−yi\Delta_{q}^{i}=x_{i}-y_{i}andΔpi=pi−pi′\Delta_{p}^{i}=p_{i}-p_{i}^{\prime}for the accumulated
discrepancies, the difference above is exactly(Δql,Δpl)(\Delta_{q}^{l},\Delta_{p}^{l}). The
following proposition bounds it in the weighted norm. See
AppendixEfor the proof.

## Proposition 5.2.

Assume the shared initial state is(q∗,p∗)∼π⊗𝒩​(0,I)(q^{*},p^{*})\sim\pi\otimes\mathcal{N}(0,I)and suppose Assumption1.1holds. If for any probability measuresν1,ν2\nu_{1},\nu_{2}onℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, we have|∫∇2V​d​ν1|ℓ∞≤C5|\int\nabla^{2}V{\rm d}\nu_{1}|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq C_{5}and|(∫∇2V​d​ν1)​(∫∇2V​d​ν2)|ℓ∞≤C6|\left(\int\nabla^{2}V{\rm d}\nu_{1}\right)\left(\int\nabla^{2}V{\rm d}\nu_{2}\right)|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq C_{6}, and the step size satisfiesh≤1−η2​βh\leq\frac{1-\eta}{2\sqrt{\beta}}, then the accumulated discretization error acrossllsteps can be bounded by(5.5)|(Δql,Δpl)|2,ℓw∞≤exp⁡[(l−1)​h​(32​C5β+116​C6β3/2+β)]​El,|(\Delta_{q}^{l},\Delta_{p}^{l})|_{2,\ell^{\infty}_{w}}\leq\exp\left[(l-1)h(\frac{3}{2}\frac{C_{5}}{\sqrt{\beta}}+\frac{1}{16}\frac{C_{6}}{\beta^{3/2}}+\sqrt{\beta})\right]E_{l}\,,

where(5.6)El=(2764​C5​h3​l+278​h2​C5β​(1−η)+38​h4​C6β​(1−η))​log⁡(2​d).E_{l}=\left(\frac{27}{64}C_{5}\,h^{3}l+{\frac{27}{8}}\frac{h^{2}C_{5}}{\sqrt{\beta}(1-\eta)}+{\frac{3}{8}}\frac{h^{4}C_{6}}{\sqrt{\beta}(1-\eta)}\right)\sqrt{\log(2d)}\,.

## 5.3.Assembling the bias bounds

Combining the discretization-error estimates above with theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}propagator bounds of Section4, under the coupling
framework of Section2, yields iterative inequalities
for HMC and its underdamped counterpart. Iterating these to the stationary limit
gives the bias bounds of Theorems1.4and1.6. The full assembly is carried out in
AppendixF.

## 6.Conclusions and Discussions

We study the delocalization of bias phenomenon in unadjusted Hamiltonian Monte
Carlo and underdamped Langevin algorithms. Although these algorithms are biased at
finite step sizehh, we show that theW2W_{2}bias of low-dimensional marginals
scales with the marginal dimension rather than the full dimensiondd, when the
target potential has weak or sparse interactions, or is Gaussian. Consequently,O​(K)O(\sqrt{K})integration steps suffice for a boundedW2W_{2}error across allKK-marginals, up to logarithmic dependence ondd. This offers a counterpoint to common practice in
Bayesian statistics, where Metropolis–Hastings adjustment is incorporated to
eliminate discretization bias. The adjusted variants instead require a step size scaling with the full dimensiondd, leading to a large number of iterations.

More broadly, we introduce a general matrix-polynomial framework for the
propagators of discretized Hamiltonian and Langevin dynamics. The polynomial
representation is well-suited to theℓ∞\ell^{\infty}norm, which is not amenable to
continuous-time tools such as differentiation. The
framework is also not specific to the leap-frog and BAOAB schemes studied here. It
applies to other splitting integrators, including OBABO and ABOBA, where, possibly
after regrouping the iterates, a single repeating core block satisfies a similar
three-term recurrence and the boundary factors contribute only bounded prefactors
that do not affect the coefficient estimates. We thus expect the framework to
facilitate the study of delocalization of bias in other dynamics as well.

Another interesting direction is randomized HMC[10,8], where the integration time
is randomized to avoid periodicity. Our proof strategy does not directly apply there, as the number of
leap-frog steps per outer loop is random rather than fixed.

We follow assumptions on the potential similar to
those in[17,30], and relaxing them toward
conditions met by real physical systems is a significant future direction. One such setting is non-log-concave target
distributions. Another is more general
interaction patterns, such as the decaying but long-range interactions of
physical systems rather than the sparse interactions considered here. These extensions are left for future work.

The appendices contain all technical details. SectionAgives the proof for Gaussian distributions. SectionBderives the matrix polynomials. SectionsCandDprovide bounds for the propagators of the leap-frog and BAOAB integrators inℓ2\ell^{2}andℓ∞\ell^{\infty}. Discretization error analysis is laid out in SectionE. The sampling bias bounds are concluded in SectionF.

## Appendix AProof for Gaussian Distributions

## Proof of Example1.2.

We assumemmis chosen so thatm​hmhavoids the discrete set of resonant values at
which some eigenmode fails to contract. Away from these, the leap-frog iteration
contracts in every mode and the chain converges toπh\pi_{h}. As these values are
exceptional, we omit the condition from the example.
According to[5], the invariant distribution for a target distributionπ=𝒩​(μ,Σ)\pi=\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)is given byπh=𝒩​(μ,Σ∞)\pi_{h}=\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma_{\infty}), whereΣ∞−1=Σ−1​(I−h24​Σ−1)\Sigma_{\infty}^{-1}=\Sigma^{-1}(I-\frac{h^{2}}{4}\Sigma^{-1}).
Consider the coupling betweenπh\pi_{h}andπ\piasX=Σ1/2​Z+μX=\Sigma^{1/2}Z+\muandY=Σ∞1/2​Z+μY=\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2}Z+\muwithZ∼𝒩​(0,I)Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I). Then,(A.1)W2,ℓ∞2​(πh,π)≤𝔼​[|X−Y|ℓ∞2]=𝔼​[|(Σ1/2−Σ∞1/2)​Z|ℓ∞2].W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}^{2}(\pi_{h},\pi)\leq\mathbb{E}[|X-Y|_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[|(\Sigma^{1/2}-\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2})Z|_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2}].

Entries of(Σ1/2−Σ∞1/2)​Z(\Sigma^{1/2}-\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2})Zare|Σ1/2−Σ∞1/2|ℓ22|\Sigma^{1/2}-\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2}|_{\ell^{2}}^{2}-subgaussian. And|Σ1/2−Σ∞1/2|ℓ2=max1≤i≤d⁡|1ωi−1ω^i|=O​(β​h2).|\Sigma^{1/2}-\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2}|_{\ell^{2}}=\max_{1\leq i\leq d}\left|\frac{1}{\omega_{i}}-\frac{1}{\hat{\omega}_{i}}\right|=O(\sqrt{\beta}h^{2})\,.

By LemmaE.1, we haveW2,ℓ∞​(πh,π)=O​(β​h2​log⁡(2​d))=O​(h​log⁡(2​d))W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}(\pi_{h},\pi)=O(\sqrt{\beta}h^{2}\sqrt{\log(2d)})=O(h\sqrt{\log(2d)})ash<1/βh<1/\sqrt{\beta}.
∎

## Appendix BDerivation of Matrix Polynomials

Throughout the appendices, when a multivariate damped Chebyshev polynomial is
evaluated at the canonical argument determined by its index, withHiH_{i}as
specified in the relevant statement, we suppress the argument and writeT~kη≔T~kη​({I−h22​Hi}i=0k−1),U~k−1η≔U~k−1η​({I−h22​Hi}i=1k−1).\tilde{T}_{k}^{\eta}\coloneqq\tilde{T}_{k}^{\eta}\big(\{I-\tfrac{h^{2}}{2}H_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k-1}\big)\,,\qquad\tilde{U}_{k-1}^{\eta}\coloneqq\tilde{U}_{k-1}^{\eta}\big(\{I-\tfrac{h^{2}}{2}H_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k-1}\big)\,.

Arguments are written explicitly whenever they differ from this convention, as in
the shifted windows of the outer-loop blocks and in the scalar reductions below.

## Proof of Proposition3.5.

Part (a).We derive the correspondence with multivariate damped matrix polynomials from three-term recurrence relations, similar to Proposition3.1but with varying Hessians.
With the same noise applied to both trajectories, the BAOAB
iteration (1.4) for the differencesΔ​qk≔xk−yk\Delta q_{k}\coloneqq x_{k}-y_{k}andΔ​pk≔pk−pk′\Delta p_{k}\coloneqq p_{k}-p^{\prime}_{k}is(B.1)Δ​qk+1\displaystyle\Delta q_{k+1}=Δ​qk+h2​(1+η)​Δ​pk−h24​(1+η)​Hk​Δ​qk,\displaystyle=\Delta q_{k}+\tfrac{h}{2}(1+\eta)\Delta p_{k}-\tfrac{h^{2}}{4}(1+\eta)H_{k}\Delta q_{k}\,,Δ​pk+1\displaystyle\Delta p_{k+1}=η​(Δ​pk−h2​Hk​Δ​qk)−h2​Hk+1​Δ​qk+1.\displaystyle=\eta\big(\Delta p_{k}-\tfrac{h}{2}H_{k}\Delta q_{k}\big)-\tfrac{h}{2}H_{k+1}\Delta q_{k+1}\,.

The leap-frog iteration (1.3) is the special caseη=1\eta=1, so
it suffices to treat (B.1) for generalη\eta. We write down (B.1) also for(Δ​qk,Δ​pk)(\Delta q_{k},\Delta p_{k})and eliminate the momentumΔ​pk\Delta p_{k}in two consecutive steps by matching its coefficients. We then obtain(B.2)Δ​qk+1=(1+η)​(I−h22​Hk)​Δ​qk−η​Δ​qk−1,k≥1.\Delta q_{k+1}=(1+\eta)(I-\tfrac{h^{2}}{2}H_{k})\Delta q_{k}-\eta\Delta q_{k-1}\,,\qquad k\geq 1\,.

Atη=1\eta=1, the
recurrence recovers the relation for the leap-frog scheme,Δ​qk+1=(2​I−h2​Hk)​Δ​qk−Δ​qk−1\Delta q_{k+1}=(2I-h^{2}H_{k})\Delta q_{k}-\Delta q_{k-1}.

We prove (3.8) by induction. It holds
fork=0,1k=0,1by the initial conditions ofT~kη\tilde{T}_{k}^{\eta}andU~kη\tilde{U}_{k}^{\eta}in Definition3.4. Fork≥1k\geq 1, the
induction hypothesis and (B.2) give(B.3)Δ​qk+1\displaystyle\Delta q_{k+1}=((1+η)​(I−h22​Hk)​T~kη−η​T~k−1η)​Δ​q0\displaystyle=\big((1+\eta)(I-\tfrac{h^{2}}{2}H_{k})\tilde{T}_{k}^{\eta}-\eta\tilde{T}_{k-1}^{\eta}\big)\Delta q_{0}+((1+η)​(I−h22​Hk)​h​U~k−1η−η​h​U~k−2η)​Δ​p0,\displaystyle\quad+\big((1+\eta)(I-\tfrac{h^{2}}{2}H_{k})h\tilde{U}_{k-1}^{\eta}-\eta h\tilde{U}_{k-2}^{\eta}\big)\Delta p_{0}\,,

and the three-term recurrences forT~kη\tilde{T}_{k}^{\eta}andU~kη\tilde{U}_{k}^{\eta}in (3.5)
and (3.6)
identify the right-hand side as (3.8),
completing the induction.

Part (b).We iterate the single-scheme identity atη=1\eta=1across the HMC outer loops.
ForN≥1N\geq 1, since both trajectories share the refreshmentξN−1\xi_{N-1}, the
identity gives(B.4)Δ​qN​m+k\displaystyle\Delta q_{Nm+k}=T~m1​({I−h22​Hi}i=(N−1)​m+kN​m+k−1)​Δ​q(N−1)​m+k\displaystyle=\tilde{T}_{m}^{1}\big(\{I-\tfrac{h^{2}}{2}H_{i}\}_{i=(N-1)m+k}^{Nm+k-1}\big)\,\Delta q_{(N-1)m+k}=∏j∈{k+i​m}i=0N−1⟵T~m1​({I−h22​Hl}l=jj+m−1)​Δ​qk,\displaystyle=\prod_{j\in\{k+im\}_{i=0}^{N-1}}^{\longleftarrow}\tilde{T}_{m}^{1}\big(\{I-\tfrac{h^{2}}{2}H_{l}\}_{l=j}^{j+m-1}\big)\,\Delta q_{k}\,,

and the conclusion follows by substituting the expression forΔ​qk\Delta q_{k}from (3.8).
∎

A property of these matrix polynomials, which we use when bounding the
propagators, is that their coefficients have alternating signs depending only on
the total order of each term.

withU~−1η≡0\tilde{U}_{-1}^{\eta}\equiv 0. Fork≥0k\geq 0,(B.7)t0(k)=1,u0(k)=12​(1+η)​ηk+1−1η−1,t_{0}^{(k)}=1\,,\qquad u_{0}^{(k)}=\tfrac{1}{2}(1+\eta)\frac{\eta^{k+1}-1}{\eta-1}\,,

together with the new order-one terms−(1+η)​h22​t0(k)​Hk-(1+\eta)\tfrac{h^{2}}{2}t_{0}^{(k)}H_{k}and−(1+η)​h22​u0(k)​Hk+1-(1+\eta)\tfrac{h^{2}}{2}u_{0}^{(k)}H_{k+1}. In each new product the prepended

To bound the propagators we need the magnitude of these coefficients. Since all
coefficients of a given order share one sign, it suffices to track their
aggregate sums(B.18)tj(k)=∑k−1≥i1>⋯>ij≥0t~i1,…,ij(k),uj(k)=∑k≥i1>⋯>ij≥1u~i1,…,ij(k).t_{j}^{(k)}=\sum_{k-1\geq i_{1}>\dots>i_{j}\geq 0}\tilde{t}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{j}}^{(k)}\,,\qquad u_{j}^{(k)}=\sum_{k\geq i_{1}>\dots>i_{j}\geq 1}\tilde{u}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{j}}^{(k)}\,.

We characterize these aggregate coefficients in two regimes, exactly whenη=1\eta=1, and by upper bounds whenη∈[0,1)\eta\in[0,1).

## Proposition B.2.

Whenη=1\eta=1,(B.19)tj(k)=k​(k+j−1)!(k−j)!​(2​j)!,k≥1,0≤j≤k,t_{j}^{(k)}=k\,\frac{(k+j-1)!}{(k-j)!(2j)!}\,,\quad k\geq 1,\ 0\leq j\leq k\,,(B.20)uj(k)=(k+j+1)!(k−j)!​(2​j+1)!,k≥0,0≤j≤k,u_{j}^{(k)}=\frac{(k+j+1)!}{(k-j)!(2j+1)!}\,,\quad k\geq 0,\ 0\leq j\leq k\,,

together witht0(0)=1t_{0}^{(0)}=1.

## Proof of PropositionB.2.

TakingHi≔IH_{i}\coloneqq Iin LemmaB.1collapses the
expansions onto their aggregate coefficients,(B.21)T~kη​({(1−h22)​I}i=0k−1)\displaystyle\tilde{T}_{k}^{\eta}(\{(1-\tfrac{h^{2}}{2})I\}_{i=0}^{k-1})=t0(k)​I+∑j=1k(−1)j​h2​j​tj(k)​I,\displaystyle=t_{0}^{(k)}I+\sum_{j=1}^{k}(-1)^{j}h^{2j}t_{j}^{(k)}I\,,U~kη​({(1−h22)​I}i=1k)\displaystyle\tilde{U}_{k}^{\eta}(\{(1-\tfrac{h^{2}}{2})I\}_{i=1}^{k})=u0(k)​I+∑j=1k(−1)j​h2​j​uj(k)​I.\displaystyle=u_{0}^{(k)}I+\sum_{j=1}^{k}(-1)^{j}h^{2j}u_{j}^{(k)}I\,.

Atη=1\eta=1with equal arguments, the multivariate polynomials reduce to the
classical Chebyshev polynomialsTkT_{k}andUkU_{k}(as noted after
Definition3.4), so(B.22)Tk​(1−h22)​I=∑j=0k(−1)j​h2​j​tj(k)​I,Uk​(1−h22)​I=∑j=0k(−1)j​h2​j​uj(k)​I.T_{k}(1-\tfrac{h^{2}}{2})\,I=\sum_{j=0}^{k}(-1)^{j}h^{2j}t_{j}^{(k)}I\,,\qquad U_{k}(1-\tfrac{h^{2}}{2})\,I=\sum_{j=0}^{k}(-1)^{j}h^{2j}u_{j}^{(k)}I\,.

The hypergeometric forms ofTkT_{k}andUkU_{k}[2]give
explicit expressions forTk​(1−x)T_{k}(1-x)andUk​(1−x)U_{k}(1-x)atx=h2/2x=h^{2}/2, and matching
coefficients yields the stated formulas.
∎

Forη∈[0,1)\eta\in[0,1), explicit formulas are no longer available, but the
following upper bounds suffice to control the propagator norms.

## Proposition B.3.

Part (b) of Proposition3.5concerns the HMC
propagators across multiple outer loops. The corresponding matrix polynomials
again have coefficients of alternating signs, related to the inner-loop
coefficientstj(k)t_{j}^{(k)}anduj(k)u_{j}^{(k)}. We only need that the outer-loop
aggregate coefficients inherit any uniform bound on the inner-loop ones.

## Appendix CBounds for Leap-frog Propagators

## C.1.ℓ2\ell^{2}bounds

Ourℓ2\ell^{2}bound forT~k1\tilde{T}_{k}^{1}adapts the discrete-time HMC contraction
result for the position component, Lemma 19 in[11], replacing the state-dependent Hessians there by
general matrices. The underlying argument carries through unchanged. Extending
this to the momentum component gives the bound forU~k−11\tilde{U}_{k-1}^{1}.

The adaptation rests on decoupling the matrix sequence from the trajectory.
Because the leap-frog scheme is nonlinear in the state-dependent Hessians, a
trajectory contraction bound|xk−yk|ℓ2≤c​|x0−y0|ℓ2|x_{k}-y_{k}|_{\ell^{2}}\leq c|x_{0}-y_{0}|_{\ell^{2}}does not by itself give a matrix-norm bound. We therefore analyze a linear system
driven by an arbitrary, state-independent matrix sequence{Hi}\{H_{i}\}. The
contraction proof of[11]does not use that the Hessians
are evaluated along the trajectory, so the adaptation is direct. The resulting
bounds hold for any initial condition and hence translate to matrix-norm bounds.
Instantiating with the integrated Hessians yields
Proposition4.1.

## Proposition C.1(ℓ2\ell^{2}bounds for leap-frog, general matrices).

Let{Hi}\{H_{i}\}be symmetric positive definite matrices withα​I⪯Hi⪯β​I\alpha I\preceq H_{i}\preceq\beta Iforα,β\alpha,\betaas in
Assumption1.1. Fork​h≤1/20​βkh\leq 1/\sqrt{20\beta}, the
matrix polynomialsT~k1\tilde{T}_{k}^{1}andU~k−11\tilde{U}_{k-1}^{1}, evaluated at the
canonical arguments{I−h22​Hi}\{I-\tfrac{h^{2}}{2}H_{i}\}with these generalHiH_{i}(recall
the shorthand introduced in
SectionB), satisfy(C.1)|T~k1|ℓ2≤1−α10​(k​h)2,|h​U~k−11|ℓ2≤25​β.|\tilde{T}_{k}^{1}|_{\ell^{2}}\leq 1-\frac{\alpha}{10}(kh)^{2}\,,\qquad|h\tilde{U}_{k-1}^{1}|_{\ell^{2}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{5\beta}}\,.

iteration withvaryingquadratic potentials, which lets us bound theirℓ2\ell^{2}norms through the contraction and boundedness of the discrete-time
dynamics, analyzed via their continuous-time interpolation.

## C.3.ℓ∞\ell^{\infty}bounds under sparse interactions

Here we use the sparsity of the Hessians to bound the propagators inℓ∞\ell^{\infty}.
The next lemma, adapted from Proposition C.1 of[17], shows that the
integrated Hessian products inherit a sparsity property from
Assumption1.5. We include the proof for
completeness.

## Lemma C.2.

SupposeVVsatisfies Assumption1.5, and letν1,…,νr\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{r}be probability measures onℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}. Then the product(C.45)(∫∇2V​d​ν1)​(∫∇2V​d​ν2)​⋯​(∫∇2V​d​νr)\left(\int\nabla^{2}V{\rm d}\nu_{1}\right)\left(\int\nabla^{2}V{\rm d}\nu_{2}\right)\cdots\left(\int\nabla^{2}V{\rm d}\nu_{r}\right)

issrs_{r}-sparse. More precisely, its(i,j)(i,j)entry is nonzero only ifj∈𝖭2​r​(i)j\in\mathsf{N}_{2r}(i).

## Proof of LemmaC.2.

The(i,j)(i,j)entry of the product is nonzero only ifj∈𝖭r​(i)j\in\mathsf{N}_{r}(i), or ifi∈𝖭r​(k)i\in\mathsf{N}_{r}(k)andj∈𝖭r​(k)j\in\mathsf{N}_{r}(k)for somekk.
Sincemax1≤i≤d⁡|𝖭2​r​(i)|=sr\max_{1\leq i\leq d}|\mathsf{N}_{2r}(i)|=s_{r}, each row has at mostsrs_{r}nonzero entries, so the matrix issrs_{r}-sparse.
∎

## Appendix DBounds for BAOAB Propagators

In this section we bound the BAOAB propagators. The key difference from the
leap-frog case is that we work with weightedℓ2\ell^{2}andℓ∞\ell^{\infty}norms,
defined through the weight matrix in (2.9), which is
what makes the propagators contractive.

## D.1.ℓ2\ell^{2}bounds

Theℓ2\ell^{2}bounds rest on the BAOAB contraction result, Theorem 5.1
of[32].

## Lemma D.1(Theorem 5.1 in[32]).

Let Assumption1.1hold. Ifh≤1−η2​βh\leq\frac{1-\eta}{2\sqrt{\beta}}, then for any initial states(x0,p0),(y0,p0′)∈ℝ2​d(x_{0},p_{0}),(y_{0},p^{\prime}_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}and any standard normal sequence{ξi}i=0k−1\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k-1}, the BAOAB chains driven by the same noise,(D.1)(xk,pk)=𝖴baoab,hξk−1​⋯​𝖴baoab,hξ0​(x0,p0),(yk,pk′)=𝖴baoab,hξk−1​⋯​𝖴baoab,hξ0​(y0,p0′),(x_{k},p_{k})=\mathsf{U}_{\text{baoab},h}^{\xi_{k-1}}\cdots\mathsf{U}_{\text{baoab},h}^{\xi_{0}}(x_{0},p_{0})\,,\quad(y_{k},p_{k}^{\prime})=\mathsf{U}_{\text{baoab},h}^{\xi_{k-1}}\cdots\mathsf{U}_{\text{baoab},h}^{\xi_{0}}(y_{0},p_{0}^{\prime})\,,

satisfy almost surely(D.2)|(xk−yk,pk−pk′)|ℓw2≤7​(1−c​(h))k−12​|(x0−y0,p0−p0′)|ℓw2,|(x_{k}-y_{k},p_{k}-p^{\prime}_{k})|_{\ell^{2}_{w}}\leq 7(1-c(h))^{\frac{k-1}{2}}|(x_{0}-y_{0},p_{0}-p^{\prime}_{0})|_{\ell^{2}_{w}}\,,

withc​(h)=α​h24​(1−η)c(h)=\frac{\alpha h^{2}}{4(1-\eta)}.

As for HMC, we prove a general-matrix version,
PropositionD.2below, by reading the polynomials with
general{Hi}\{H_{i}\}as BAOAB propagators with varying quadratic potentials and
turning the contraction of LemmaD.1into
operator-norm bounds.

## Proposition D.2(ℓ2\ell^{2}bounds for BAOAB, general matrices).

Let{Hi}\{H_{i}\}be symmetric positive definite withα​I⪯Hi⪯β​I\alpha I\preceq H_{i}\preceq\beta Iforα,β\alpha,\betaas in
Assumption1.1. Forh≤1−η2​βh\leq\frac{1-\eta}{2\sqrt{\beta}}andc​(h)=α​h24​(1−η)c(h)=\frac{\alpha h^{2}}{4(1-\eta)}, the polynomialsT~kη\tilde{T}_{k}^{\eta}andU~k−1η\tilde{U}_{k-1}^{\eta}defined with{Hi}\{H_{i}\}, and the full-space weighted
propagatorMbaoab,kwM_{\text{baoab},k}^{w}, satisfy(D.3)|Mbaoab,kw|ℓ2≤7​(1−c​(h))k−12,|M_{\text{baoab},k}^{w}|_{\ell^{2}}\leq 7(1-c(h))^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\,,(D.4)|T~kη|ℓ2≤7​2​(1−c​(h))k−12,1a−b2​|−b​T~kη+h​U~k−1η|ℓ2≤7​2​(1−c​(h))k−12.|\tilde{T}_{k}^{\eta}|_{\ell^{2}}\leq 7\sqrt{2}(1-c(h))^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\,,\quad\frac{1}{\sqrt{a-b^{2}}}\left|-b\tilde{T}_{k}^{\eta}+h\tilde{U}_{k-1}^{\eta}\right|_{\ell^{2}}\leq 7\sqrt{2}\left(1-c(h)\right)^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\,.

induction gives(D.7)Δ​qk=T~kη​Δ​q0+h​U~k−1η​Δ​p0,\Delta q_{k}=\tilde{T}_{k}^{\eta}\Delta q_{0}+h\tilde{U}_{k-1}^{\eta}\Delta p_{0}\,,

is precisely (D.3). For the position
blocks, the norm equivalence (2.10) gives(D.10)|Δ​qk|ℓ2≤|Δ​qk|ℓ22+a​|Δ​pk|ℓ22≤2​|(Δ​qk,Δ​pk)|ℓw2,|\Delta q_{k}|_{\ell^{2}}\leq\sqrt{|\Delta q_{k}|_{\ell^{2}}^{2}+a|\Delta p_{k}|_{\ell^{2}}^{2}}\leq\sqrt{2}|(\Delta q_{k},\Delta p_{k})|_{\ell^{2}_{w}}\,,

so(D.11)|Δ​qk|ℓ2≤7​2​(1−c​(h))k−12​|W​(Δ​q0,Δ​p0)|ℓ2.|\Delta q_{k}|_{\ell^{2}}\leq 7\sqrt{2}(1-c(h))^{\frac{k-1}{2}}|W(\Delta q_{0},\Delta p_{0})|_{\ell^{2}}\,.

## D.2.ℓ∞\ell^{\infty}bounds under weak interactions

We bound the BAOAB propagators inℓ∞\ell^{\infty}following[32], decomposing the scheme into the operators𝖠𝖡𝖠𝖮\mathsf{A}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{A}\mathsf{O},𝖡𝖠𝖮\mathsf{B}\mathsf{A}\mathsf{O}, and𝖠𝖡\mathsf{A}\mathsf{B}as in (D.8), where𝖠𝖡𝖠𝖮\mathsf{A}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{A}\mathsf{O}drives the contraction and the boundary
terms𝖡𝖠𝖮\mathsf{B}\mathsf{A}\mathsf{O},𝖠𝖡\mathsf{A}\mathsf{B}stay bounded. The contraction remains under the weak interactions.

Unlike HMC, a diagonal Hessian does not give a diagonal propagator here. On the
joint(q,p)(q,p)space the propagator is a2×22\times 2block matrix with diagonal
blocks. The crude inequality|M|ℓ∞≤2​|M|ℓ2|M|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq\sqrt{2}|M|_{\ell^{2}}(from
the two nonzero entries per row) would not preserve theℓ2\ell^{2}contraction, so
we bound each block inℓ∞\ell^{\infty}directly, using theℓ2\ell^{2}bounds as a
guide to the contraction rate.

the(1,2)(1,2)block by2​β3​(h2+h4⋅2⋅14​β⋅2120​β)≤101160​3\frac{2\sqrt{\beta}}{\sqrt{3}}(\tfrac{h}{2}+\tfrac{h}{4}\cdot 2\cdot\tfrac{1}{4\beta}\cdot\tfrac{21}{20}\beta)\leq\frac{101}{160\sqrt{3}},
the(2,1)(2,1)block by12​1β​h⋅2120​β≤2180\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\beta}}h\cdot\tfrac{21}{20}\beta\leq\tfrac{21}{80},

By the same block-wise bounding, the(1,1)(1,1)block is at most1+h4​β​2120​β≤1811601+\tfrac{h}{4\sqrt{\beta}}\tfrac{21}{20}\beta\leq\tfrac{181}{160}, the(1,2)(1,2)block at most2​β3​(h2+h2​14​β​2120​β)≤101160​3\frac{2\sqrt{\beta}}{\sqrt{3}}(\tfrac{h}{2}+\tfrac{h}{2}\tfrac{1}{4\beta}\tfrac{21}{20}\beta)\leq\frac{101}{160\sqrt{3}},

A row sum concludes the proof.

## Appendix EDiscretization Error Analysis

We now bound the discretization error by comparing the discrete and continuous
dynamics. The following two bounds from[17], used
throughout, produce thelog⁡(2​d)\log(2d)dependence of the bias.

## Lemma E.1(Lemma B.1 in[17]).

SupposeY=(Y(1),…,Y(d))∈ℝdY=(Y^{(1)},\dots,Y^{(d)})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}with eachY(i)Y^{(i)}centered and
sub-Gaussian with variance proxyσ2\sigma^{2}, i.e.𝔼​[exp⁡(λ​Y(i))]≤exp⁡(12​λ2​σ2)\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda Y^{(i)})]\leq\exp(\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}). Then(E.1)𝔼​[|Y|ℓ∞2]≤4​σ2​log⁡(2​d).\mathbb{E}[|Y|_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2}]\leq 4\sigma^{2}\log(2d)\,.

## Lemma E.2(Proposition 2.3 in[17]).

Let Assumption1.1hold. Then(E.2)𝔼π​[|∇V|ℓ∞2]≤2​β​log⁡(2​d).\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[|\nabla V|_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2}]}\leq 2\sqrt{\beta\log(2d)}\,.

## E.1.Discretization error in HMC

The discretization error
decompositions (2.6)
and (2.7) in
Section2.2share the common structure(E.3)|M​Π1​𝖴hmc,hm−i−1​(𝖴hmc,h1−𝖴hmch)​(q∗,p∗)|2,ℓ∞,|M\mathsf{\Pi}_{1}\mathsf{U}_{\text{hmc},h}^{m-i-1}(\mathsf{U}_{\text{hmc},h}^{1}-\mathsf{U}_{\text{hmc}}^{h})(q^{*},p^{*})|_{2,\ell^{\infty}}\,,

with a matrixM∈ℝd×dM\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}and(q∗,p∗)∼π⊗𝒩​(0,I)(q^{*},p^{*})\sim\pi\otimes\mathcal{N}(0,I),
where, as recalled in Section5,(E.4)(𝖴hmc,h1−𝖴hmch)​(q∗,p∗)=((𝖰h1−𝖰h),(𝖯h1−𝖯h))​(q∗,p∗).(\mathsf{U}_{\text{hmc},h}^{1}-\mathsf{U}_{\text{hmc}}^{h})(q^{*},p^{*})=((\mathsf{Q}_{h}^{1}-\mathsf{Q}^{h}),(\mathsf{P}_{h}^{1}-\mathsf{P}^{h}))(q^{*},p^{*})\,.

The following proposition bounds the error of each component.

## Proposition E.3(Generalization of Proposition5.1).

For any matrixM∈ℝd×dM\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}, suppose there exist constantsC3,C4>0C_{3},C_{4}>0(possibly depending onβ\beta) such that|M​∫∇2V​d​ν1|ℓ∞<C3|M\int\nabla^{2}V{\rm d}\nu_{1}|_{\ell^{\infty}}\allowbreak<C_{3}and|M​(∫∇2V​d​ν1)​(∫∇2V​d​ν2)|ℓ∞<C4|M(\int\nabla^{2}V{\rm d}\nu_{1})(\int\nabla^{2}V{\rm d}\nu_{2})|_{\ell^{\infty}}<C_{4}for all probability measuresν1,ν2\nu_{1},\nu_{2}onℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}. Then forh≤1/20​βh\leq 1/\sqrt{20\beta}and(q∗,p∗)∼π⊗𝒩​(0,I)(q^{*},p^{*})\sim\pi\otimes\mathcal{N}(0,I),(E.5)|M​(𝖰h1​(q∗,p∗)−𝖰h​(q∗,p∗))|2,ℓ∞≤12​h3​C3​log⁡(2​d),|M(\mathsf{Q}_{h}^{1}(q^{*},p^{*})-\mathsf{Q}^{h}(q^{*},p^{*}))|_{2,\ell^{\infty}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}h^{3}C_{3}\sqrt{\log(2d)}\,,(E.6)|M​(𝖯h1​(q∗,p∗)−𝖯h​(q∗,p∗))|2,ℓ∞≤2​h2​C3​log⁡(2​d)+12​5​h4​C4​log⁡(2​d).|M(\mathsf{P}_{h}^{1}(q^{*},p^{*})-\mathsf{P}^{h}(q^{*},p^{*}))|_{2,\ell^{\infty}}\leq 2h^{2}C_{3}\sqrt{\log(2d)}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{5}}h^{4}C_{4}\sqrt{\log(2d)}\,.

Since(q∗,p∗)∼π⊗𝒩​(0,I)(q^{*},p^{*})\sim\pi\otimes\mathcal{N}(0,I), stationarity givesqt∼πq_{t}\sim\pifor alltt, so by LemmaE.1,(E.10)𝔼​∫0h∫0t|qs−q∗|ℓ∞2​ds​dt≤23​h4​log⁡(2​d)+160​h6​𝔼π​[|∇V|ℓ∞2].\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{h}\int_{0}^{t}|q_{s}-q^{*}|_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2}{\rm d}s{\rm d}t\leq\tfrac{2}{3}h^{4}\log(2d)+\tfrac{1}{60}h^{6}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[|\nabla V|_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2}]\,.

which is (E.5). ## E.2.Discretization error in UL

We adapt the framework of[32], in particular the
analysis leading to their Proposition 8.3, to theW2,ℓ∞W_{2,\ell^{\infty}}norm. We use
an HOH chain preserving the invariant distribution exactly, and measure the
discrepancy between the BAOAB and HOH chains.

## Proposition E.4.

Consider initial states(x,p)(x,p)and(y,p′)(y,p^{\prime})with(x,p)∼π⊗𝒩​(0,I)(x,p)\sim\pi\otimes\mathcal{N}(0,I). Apply one HOH step to(x,p)(x,p)and one
BAOAB step to(y,p′)(y,p^{\prime}), coupled through the same Gaussian noiseξ\xi, and set(Δq,Δp)≔𝖴hoh,hξ​(x,p)−𝖴baoab,hξ​(y,p′)(\Delta_{q},\Delta_{p})\coloneqq\mathsf{U}_{\text{hoh},h}^{\xi}(x,p)-\mathsf{U}_{\text{baoab},h}^{\xi}(y,p^{\prime}).
Supposeπ\pisatisfies Assumption1.1andh≤1−η2​βh\leq\frac{1-\eta}{2\sqrt{\beta}}.
